@@776988 <h> High Arctic Research Station Forced To Close <p> CBC <p> Posted : 02/28/2012 2:27 pm EST Updated : 04/29/2012 5:12 am EDT <p> Polar Environment Atmospheric Research Laboratory ( PEARL ) , is shown shrouded in fog , July 24 , 2006 in the Canadian territory of Nunavut near the Eureka Weather Station . When the sky is free from clouds , the facility allows scientists to use a variety of instruments to study ozone , air quality and climate change in Canadas high Arctic . CP <p> Canada 's northernmost research laboratory is shutting down due to lack of funding . <p> The Polar Environment Atmospheric Research Laboratory ( PEARL ) in Eureka , Nunavut , which made key measurements last winter used to detect and analyze the largest ozone hole ever detected over the Arctic , will cease year-round operations on April 30 . At that time , its equipment will be removed and the building will remain available only for intermittent , short-term projects . <p> " When you run out of money , there 's no alternative but to close the lab , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the principal investigator for PEARL , said Tuesday . <p> The station has been tracking ozone depletion , air quality and climate change in the High Arctic since 2005 . But the Canadian Network for Detection of Atmospheric Change , an informal network of university researchers that runs the station , has n't been able to secure the $1.5 million annual funding required to continue running the station all year round . <p> That is largely due to the discontinuation of government funding to the Canadian Foundation for Climate and Atmospheric Sciences , which had been covering three-quarters of the station 's costs , and the end to the International Polar Year program . <p> Drummond said the network has since applied for various government funding programs and has been turned down for all of them , despite the government 's frequent assertion that the Arctic is a priority for Canada . <p> PEARL is the biggest lab in Canada 's High Arctic and , at 80 degrees north latitude , one of the closest in the world to the North Pole . <p> " Shutting it down causes a big @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We 're losing the ability to know what 's going on up there . " <p> Many environmental changes happening on a global scale , such as many related to climate , are occurring first in the Arctic , he said , adding " this is an early warning system that we 're letting go . " <p> The network said it is trying to pursue funding for short-term research campaigns that would keep the station running for part of the year . In the meantime , it is making intermittent scientific measurements in the summer . <p> However , an end to year-round operations means the station can no longer take measurements during the polar night -- the prolonged , weeks-long darkness of the Arctic winter -- and contribute the data it has been collecting for international measurements of aerosols , atmospheric composition and carbon . <h> ' Essential ' measurements <p> Matthias Schneider , a German researcher who leads a global network that uses data from around the world to understand atmospheric water cycle and its role in climate , said PEARL 's closure will eliminate a " unique @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the global effort . <p> University of Toronto researcher Kimberly Strong said the end to those and other measurements come " just as our need for high-quality data in the changing Arctic is becoming ever more important . " <p> The closure could also scuttle plans for a polar telescope and magnetic observatory at the site . <p> The government is expected to set up a new High Arctic research station 1,300 kilometres south of PEARL in Cambridge Bay . However , that wo n't happen until 2017 , and because it is so much farther south , Drummond does n't think it would be able to make up for the loss of PEARL . <p> Environment Minister Peter Kent points out that his department does provide some direct support worth about $250,000 toward the polar lab 's $1.5-million annual budget . But he says the scientists failed to get grants for the rest of the money needed to keep it going . <p> " We certainly continue to support the concept of PEARL , but I certainly as minister of Environment Canada do not have a million and a half @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@777032 <h> Incompetent people lack the ability to know when they 're performing badly , Cornell University researcher finds , thereby unlocking the mystery of the universe . <p> It turns out that incompetent people are too incompetent to recognize their own incompetence . <p> Luckily , we do n't just have to take the incompetent people 's word for it -- there 's years of rigorous study to back this up . <p> For more than a decade , David Dunning , a psychologist at Cornell University , has found in his research that it 's " intrinsically difficult to get a sense of what we do n't know . " <p> Dunning , working with Justin Kruger , a former colleague at Cornell now at New York University , told Life 's Little Mysteries , that in their studies , they give people a short test , tally their scores , then ask the subjects how they think they did . <p> People who did n't do well on the test are only slightly less confident about their ability than those who performed well . <p> And @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who did very poorly on the test . <p> " People at the bottom still think they 're outperforming other people , " Dunning said . <p> It does n't matter what the test is about -- logical reasoning , how to avoid sexually transmitted diseases , grammar , the funniness of jokes . <p> Even when Dunning and his colleagues offer a $100 reward to those who can rate themselves accurately , study participants just ca n't do it . <p> This inherent inability to accurately gauge our own level of knowledge may be an underlying cause of many of society 's ills , including climate change denialism , Dunning said . <p> " Many people do n't have training in science , and so they may very well misunderstand the science . But because they do n't have the knowledge to evaluate it , they do n't realize how off their evaluations might be , " he said . <p> Stay tuned . There 's more to come . Dunning 's related interest : " how people bolster their sense of self-worth by carefully tailoring the judgments they make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ website . <p> " That is , people tend to make judgments of others that reflect favorably back on themselves , doing so even when the self is not under explicit scrutiny . "
